Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is work stuff, mostly. I work in meat processing, and there are all these sanitation steps and batch logs and end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll start them—I know how to do them—but then somehow I leave out the last parts.

And it’s not like I don’t care. I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. It’s just… I get through most of it and then it’s like my brain has already moved on. Like yesterday I was thinking about whether I’d taken chicken out of my freezer at home, which isn’t even relevant, and then later I realized I hadn’t finished the paperwork. It’s been happening enough that I’m worried.

Yeah… yeah, I think it is. I mean, I try not to be dramatic about it, because everybody messes up paperwork sometimes, but it’s gotten to where I’ve had formal retraining meetings at work. Like sitting down and going over the steps again, even though I already know them, which feels awful.

Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.

It’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.

I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been a serious problem since I switched into full-time meat processing about two years and four months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.

And any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.

So yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.

Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.

Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.

swear I just had them in my hand before I spend ten minutes retracing my steps.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n I remember understanding the lessons but missing chunks of instructions unless the teacher stayed close to me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work this has led to formal retraining meetings, coworkers covering parts of my duties, and real fear that I’ll be moved off the line or fired. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been a serious problem since I switched into full-time meat processing about two years and four months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is work stuff, mostly. I work in meat processing, and there are all these sanitation steps and batch logs and end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll start them—I know how to do them—but then somehow I leave out the last parts.\n\nAnd it’s not like I don’t care. I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. It’s just… I get through most of it and then it’s like my brain has already moved on. Like yesterday I was thinking about whether I’d taken chicken out of my freezer at home, which isn’t even relevant, and then later I realized I hadn’t finished the paperwork. It’s been happening enough that I’m worried.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside work, probably, but the thing I’m most worried about is at work because it’s so obvious there. Like I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch log, and I really do know the order—I’ve done it enough times—and then I’ll get almost to the end and somehow just not finish the last parts.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to skip it. I know it matters, and I know my job depends on it being complete. But my brain kind of… wanders off before the task is actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family because of this issue with not finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… relationships? I don’t know. I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a big way. My family will get annoyed if I say I’ll do something and then don’t get around to finishing it, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nLike, my mom will remind me about stuff and then I’ll be like, “Yeah, I know,” and then somehow we’re talking about groceries or whether the dog ate something weird, and I’ve completely lost track of the original thing. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused, like, major relationship problems. Or… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with not finishing tasks is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it is. I mean, I try not to be dramatic about it, because everybody messes up paperwork sometimes, but it’s gotten to where I’ve had formal retraining meetings at work. Like sitting down and going over the steps again, even though I already know them, which feels awful.\n\nAnd then my coworkers end up covering parts of what I was supposed to finish, and I hate that because I don’t want to be that person on the line. I want to be reliable. I keep thinking about being moved off the line or fired, and then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to make dinner or whatever.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting me. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone people can count on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. I just keep feeling like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone they can trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that just happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.\n\nAnd then like a minute later, I realize I only really held onto the first part. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. Then I have to ask someone else, “Wait, what did she say after that?” which is embarrassing because I was literally right there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. Like, the big thing is work, honestly. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.0, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is work stuff, mostly. I work in meat processing, and there are all these sanitation steps and batch logs and end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll start them—I know how to do them—but then somehow I leave out the last parts.\n\nAnd it’s not like I don’t care. I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. It’s just… I get through most of it and then it’s like my brain has already moved on. Like yesterday I was thinking about whether I’d taken chicken out of my freezer at home, which isn’t even relevant, and then later I realized I hadn’t finished the paperwork. It’s been happening enough that I’m worried.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside work, probably, but the thing I’m most worried about is at work because it’s so obvious there. Like I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch log, and I really do know the order—I’ve done it enough times—and then I’ll get almost to the end and somehow just not finish the last parts.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to skip it. I know it matters, and I know my job depends on it being complete. But my brain kind of… wanders off before the task is actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family because of this issue with not finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… relationships? I don’t know. I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a big way. My family will get annoyed if I say I’ll do something and then don’t get around to finishing it, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nLike, my mom will remind me about stuff and then I’ll be like, “Yeah, I know,” and then somehow we’re talking about groceries or whether the dog ate something weird, and I’ve completely lost track of the original thing. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused, like, major relationship problems. Or… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with not finishing tasks is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it is. I mean, I try not to be dramatic about it, because everybody messes up paperwork sometimes, but it’s gotten to where I’ve had formal retraining meetings at work. Like sitting down and going over the steps again, even though I already know them, which feels awful.\n\nAnd then my coworkers end up covering parts of what I was supposed to finish, and I hate that because I don’t want to be that person on the line. I want to be reliable. I keep thinking about being moved off the line or fired, and then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to make dinner or whatever.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting me. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone people can count on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. I just keep feeling like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone they can trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that just happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.\n\nAnd then like a minute later, I realize I only really held onto the first part. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. Then I have to ask someone else, “Wait, what did she say after that?” which is embarrassing because I was literally right there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. Like, the big thing is work, honestly. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I keep thinking about being moved off the line or fired, and then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to make dinner or whatever.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting me. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone people can count on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. I just keep feeling like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone they can trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that just happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.\n\nAnd then like a minute later, I realize I only really held onto the first part. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. Then I have to ask someone else, “Wait, what did she say after that?” which is embarrassing because I was literally right there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. Like, the big thing is work, honestly. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with finishing tasks and remembering things are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work it is, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s gotten to the point where I’ve had formal retraining meetings about stuff I should already know, and coworkers end up covering parts of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I start worrying, like, are they going to move me off the line, or fire me? Which is embarrassing to even say. I want to be reliable, you know? But sometimes it feels like I’m becoming more of a safety concern than someone they can count on.\n\nDaily life is… I don’t know, that’s harder to measure, because everyone forgets laundry or loses track of errands sometimes. I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. But it doesn’t feel like that’s the only reason.

Even when I come in for a shift telling myself, like, okay, today I’m going to really stay locked in, after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels just sort of… blur together. And then I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or the same log sheet, but I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.

It’s weird because I’m there, I’m looking at it, but my brain isn’t staying with it. And then I’ll snap back and be like, wait, did I already do this part, or was I about to do it? So yeah, the worry is there, but I think something else is happening with my focus too.

I work in meat processing, and there are all these sanitation steps and batch logs and end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll start them—I know how to do them—but then somehow I leave out the last parts.\n\nAnd it’s not like I don’t care. I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. It’s just… I get through most of it and then it’s like my brain has already moved on. Like yesterday I was thinking about whether I’d taken chicken out of my freezer at home, which isn’t even relevant, and then later I realized I hadn’t finished the paperwork. It’s been happening enough that I’m worried.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside work, probably, but the thing I’m most worried about is at work because it’s so obvious there. Like I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch log, and I really do know the order—I’ve done it enough times—and then I’ll get almost to the end and somehow just not finish the last parts.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to skip it. I know it matters, and I know my job depends on it being complete. But my brain kind of… wanders off before the task is actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family because of this issue with not finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… relationships? I don’t know. I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a big way. My family will get annoyed if I say I’ll do something and then don’t get around to finishing it, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nLike, my mom will remind me about stuff and then I’ll be like, “Yeah, I know,” and then somehow we’re talking about groceries or whether the dog ate something weird, and I’ve completely lost track of the original thing. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused, like, major relationship problems. Or… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with not finishing tasks is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it is. I mean, I try not to be dramatic about it, because everybody messes up paperwork sometimes, but it’s gotten to where I’ve had formal retraining meetings at work. Like sitting down and going over the steps again, even though I already know them, which feels awful.\n\nAnd then my coworkers end up covering parts of what I was supposed to finish, and I hate that because I don’t want to be that person on the line. I want to be reliable. I keep thinking about being moved off the line or fired, and then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to make dinner or whatever.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting me. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone people can count on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. I just keep feeling like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone they can trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that just happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.\n\nAnd then like a minute later, I realize I only really held onto the first part. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. Then I have to ask someone else, “Wait, what did she say after that?” which is embarrassing because I was literally right there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. Like, the big thing is work, honestly. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with finishing tasks and remembering things are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work it is, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s gotten to the point where I’ve had formal retraining meetings about stuff I should already know, and coworkers end up covering parts of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I start worrying, like, are they going to move me off the line, or fire me? Which is embarrassing to even say. I want to be reliable, you know? But sometimes it feels like I’m becoming more of a safety concern than someone they can count on.\n\nDaily life is… I don’t know, that’s harder to measure, because everyone forgets laundry or loses track of errands sometimes. But at work, yeah, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to finish tasks if you had someone reminding you or helping you stay on track?\n\nPatient: Maybe? Like, if someone was right there reminding me, I think I’d do better, but that’s also kind of the problem. I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch logs, and I know what the ending is supposed to be, but then somehow I leave out the last part.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s not like I don’t understand the job. I can be doing the end-of-line paperwork and then I think, okay, I need to check one more thing, and then someone asks me something or I notice a tray is out of place, and suddenly I’ve walked away like it’s finished when it isn’t.\n\nSo yeah, reminders would probably help, but I hate that I’d need that much watching just to finish things that I know matter. My job kind of depends on doing those last pieces correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about work are causing you to have trouble finishing tasks, or do you think there's something else going on that's making it hard for you to focus?\n\nPatient: I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. But it doesn’t feel like that’s the only reason.\n\nEven when I come in for a shift telling myself, like, okay, today I’m going to really stay locked in, after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels just sort of… blur together. And then I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or the same log sheet, but I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m there, I’m looking at it, but my brain isn’t staying with it. And then I’ll snap back and be like, wait, did I already do this part, or was I about to do it? So yeah, the worry is there, but I think something else is happening with my focus too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My job kind of depends on doing those last pieces correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about work are causing you to have trouble finishing tasks, or do you think there's something else going on that's making it hard for you to focus?\n\nPatient: I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… outside of work, it’s harder for me to say clearly. Like, I’ll forget little things at home and then start thinking about whether that counts or if I’m just being messy, and then I end up comparing it to work because that’s where it’s really obvious.

At work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve had those formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to step in and cover parts of what I was supposed to finish. And that makes me feel awful, because I don’t want people thinking they have to babysit me on the line.

I guess in my personal life it bothers me mostly because I carry that stress home. I keep thinking, what if they move me off the line, or what if I get fired? I feel like I’m starting to look like a safety concern instead of someone reliable, and that’s… yeah, that really gets to me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess what brings me in is work stuff, mostly. I work in meat processing, and there are all these sanitation steps and batch logs and end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll start them—I know how to do them—but then somehow I leave out the last parts.\n\nAnd it’s not like I don’t care. I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. It’s just… I get through most of it and then it’s like my brain has already moved on. Like yesterday I was thinking about whether I’d taken chicken out of my freezer at home, which isn’t even relevant, and then later I realized I hadn’t finished the paperwork. It’s been happening enough that I’m worried.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… outside work, probably, but the thing I’m most worried about is at work because it’s so obvious there. Like I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch log, and I really do know the order—I’ve done it enough times—and then I’ll get almost to the end and somehow just not finish the last parts.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m trying to skip it. I know it matters, and I know my job depends on it being complete. But my brain kind of… wanders off before the task is actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family because of this issue with not finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um… relationships? I don’t know. I mean, not that I’ve really noticed in a big way. My family will get annoyed if I say I’ll do something and then don’t get around to finishing it, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nLike, my mom will remind me about stuff and then I’ll be like, “Yeah, I know,” and then somehow we’re talking about groceries or whether the dog ate something weird, and I’ve completely lost track of the original thing. But I wouldn’t say it’s caused, like, major relationship problems. Or… I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this issue with not finishing tasks is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think it is. I mean, I try not to be dramatic about it, because everybody messes up paperwork sometimes, but it’s gotten to where I’ve had formal retraining meetings at work. Like sitting down and going over the steps again, even though I already know them, which feels awful.\n\nAnd then my coworkers end up covering parts of what I was supposed to finish, and I hate that because I don’t want to be that person on the line. I want to be reliable. I keep thinking about being moved off the line or fired, and then I go home and I’m still thinking about it while I’m trying to make dinner or whatever.\n\nSo yeah, it’s affecting me. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone people can count on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about making mistakes at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I worry about it a lot—probably more than I let on at work. Because it’s not just like, “Oh, I forgot a small thing,” it turns into meetings where they retrain me on stuff I already know, and then I feel embarrassed sitting there like I’m brand new.\n\nAnd then if I miss finishing something, someone else has to cover part of my job, and I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking they have to babysit me or double-check everything I touch. I start thinking, like, what if they move me off the line, or what if this is the thing that gets me fired?\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll be doing something totally normal later, like making food at home, and I’m still replaying whether I messed up at work. I just keep feeling like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of someone they can trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that just happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. Like, especially when someone tells me something out loud. My supervisor will give me directions face-to-face, and I’ll be standing there nodding like, okay, yes, I’m getting this.\n\nAnd then like a minute later, I realize I only really held onto the first part. The rest is just… gone, or fuzzy. Then I have to ask someone else, “Wait, what did she say after that?” which is embarrassing because I was literally right there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying not to listen. I’ll think I am listening, and then my brain just keeps one piece and drops the rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering instructions affects how you interact with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I mean… with friends or family, I guess it comes out mostly because I’m already stressed from work. Like, the big thing is work, honestly. That’s where it’s actually caused problems—formal retraining meetings and coworkers having to finish or cover pieces of what I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd then I bring that home in my head, so if someone in my family asks me something, I might be kind of distracted because I’m thinking, “Am I going to get moved off the line? Are they going to fire me?” It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m not dependable.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about friends and family. I just keep coming back to work because that’s where it feels the worst. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable person I want to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid starting tasks at work because you're worried about not being able to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not every single task, but the ones where I know it’s going to be a lot of reading or rules, I’ll avoid starting them. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with finishing tasks and remembering things are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work it is, honestly. Like the certification modules at work—I put those off until the absolute last minute.\n\nAnd any long procedure reading, too. I know it’s important, especially with safety stuff, but sitting there trying to actually absorb all of it feels… I don’t know, almost physically exhausting. Like I’ll open it and then suddenly I’m thinking I should clean my locker or check something else first, which is ridiculous because it doesn’t make the module go away.\n\nSo yeah, I avoid starting those because I’m worried I’m not going to get through it or actually retain it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work that you think might be related to your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work, I think it mostly shows up because work is so bad right now. Like I’ll be at home but still thinking about what happened on the line, or whether people had to cover for me again.\n\nI know you asked about personal relationships and daily life, but honestly the biggest impact is at work. I’ve had formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to take over parts of what I was supposed to do. And then I go home feeling like… I don’t know, like I’m becoming a safety problem instead of someone people can count on.\n\nSo maybe it affects home because I’m carrying that fear around—like, am I going to get moved off the line, or fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with finishing tasks and remembering things are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work it is, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s gotten to the point where I’ve had formal retraining meetings about stuff I should already know, and coworkers end up covering parts of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I start worrying, like, are they going to move me off the line, or fire me? Which is embarrassing to even say. I want to be reliable, you know? But sometimes it feels like I’m becoming more of a safety concern than someone they can count on.\n\nDaily life is… I don’t know, that’s harder to measure, because everyone forgets laundry or loses track of errands sometimes. But at work, yeah, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to finish tasks if you had someone reminding you or helping you stay on track?\n\nPatient: Maybe? Like, if someone was right there reminding me, I think I’d do better, but that’s also kind of the problem. I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch logs, and I know what the ending is supposed to be, but then somehow I leave out the last part.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s not like I don’t understand the job. I can be doing the end-of-line paperwork and then I think, okay, I need to check one more thing, and then someone asks me something or I notice a tray is out of place, and suddenly I’ve walked away like it’s finished when it isn’t.\n\nSo yeah, reminders would probably help, but I hate that I’d need that much watching just to finish things that I know matter. My job kind of depends on doing those last pieces correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about work are causing you to have trouble finishing tasks, or do you think there's something else going on that's making it hard for you to focus?\n\nPatient: I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. But it doesn’t feel like that’s the only reason.\n\nEven when I come in for a shift telling myself, like, okay, today I’m going to really stay locked in, after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels just sort of… blur together. And then I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or the same log sheet, but I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m there, I’m looking at it, but my brain isn’t staying with it. I’ve had those formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to step in and cover parts of what I was supposed to finish. I keep thinking, what if they move me off the line, or what if I get fired? It makes me kind of ashamed and distracted around people.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friends because of your difficulty finishing tasks or remembering things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve, like… lost a friend outright because of it. Not that I know of.\n\nI mean, I worry people get annoyed with me, and then I start thinking about whether I forgot to text someone back or follow through on something, but I can’t point to someone and say, “Yeah, they stopped being my friend because of that.” Most of my brain goes to work stuff lately, honestly, so I might not even be tracking that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties at work have affected your relationships with friends or family in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s been significant with friends or family. Like, I don’t think anyone has sat me down and said, “You’re being distant,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’m quieter after work, and then my family will ask if I’m okay, but then I’m thinking about the whole day and whether I messed something up, and also there’s always just normal life stuff—like laundry or dinner or whatever—so it’s hard to separate.\n\nSo… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m distracted or stressed, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s really affected the relationships themselves in a big way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. Like, I get upset and kind of down about how things are going, especially after a bad shift, but then I start thinking about, like, did I label something wrong, did someone have to fix it, and then it turns into this whole spiral about work.\n\nSo… sad sometimes, yeah, but I’m not sure if that’s the same as being depressed. I guess I’m not totally sure what you mean by often.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Um… most of the day? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I feel empty, exactly. Sad comes and goes, I guess, but it’s not like I wake up and the whole day is just… that.\n\nSometimes I’ll feel kind of low, especially if something at work is hanging over me, and then I’ll get distracted by, like, what I need to do when I get home or whether I remembered to take something out of the freezer—sorry, that’s random—but yeah, I don’t think I feel sad or empty most of the day. At least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with finishing tasks and remembering things are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think at work it is, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s gotten to the point where I’ve had formal retraining meetings about stuff I should already know, and coworkers end up covering parts of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd then I start worrying, like, are they going to move me off the line, or fire me? Which is embarrassing to even say. I want to be reliable, you know? But sometimes it feels like I’m becoming more of a safety concern than someone they can count on.\n\nDaily life is… I don’t know, that’s harder to measure, because everyone forgets laundry or loses track of errands sometimes. But at work, yeah, it’s definitely interfering.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to finish tasks if you had someone reminding you or helping you stay on track?\n\nPatient: Maybe? Like, if someone was right there reminding me, I think I’d do better, but that’s also kind of the problem. I’ll start the sanitation steps or the batch logs, and I know what the ending is supposed to be, but then somehow I leave out the last part.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s not like I don’t understand the job. I can be doing the end-of-line paperwork and then I think, okay, I need to check one more thing, and then someone asks me something or I notice a tray is out of place, and suddenly I’ve walked away like it’s finished when it isn’t.\n\nSo yeah, reminders would probably help, but I hate that I’d need that much watching just to finish things that I know matter. My job kind of depends on doing those last pieces correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about work are causing you to have trouble finishing tasks, or do you think there's something else going on that's making it hard for you to focus?\n\nPatient: I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. And then I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or the same log sheet, but I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m there, I’m looking at it, but my brain isn’t staying with it. And then I’ll snap back and be like, wait, did I already do this part, or was I about to do it? So yeah, the worry is there, but I think something else is happening with my focus too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with tasks and memory are causing problems in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, it’s harder for me to say clearly. Like, I’ll forget little things at home and then start thinking about whether that counts or if I’m just being messy, and then I end up comparing it to work because that’s where it’s really obvious.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about work are causing you to have trouble finishing tasks, or do you think there's something else going on that's making it hard for you to focus?\n\nPatient: I think the worrying makes it worse, probably, because then I’m thinking about whether I’m messing up, which is its own whole loop. But it doesn’t feel like that’s the only reason.\n\nEven when I come in for a shift telling myself, like, okay, today I’m going to really stay locked in, after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels just sort of… blur together. And then I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or the same log sheet, but I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m there, I’m looking at it, but my brain isn’t staying with it. And then I’ll snap back and be like, wait, did I already do this part, or was I about to do it? So yeah, the worry is there, but I think something else is happening with my focus too.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with tasks and memory are causing problems in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, it’s harder for me to say clearly. Like, I’ll forget little things at home and then start thinking about whether that counts or if I’m just being messy, and then I end up comparing it to work because that’s where it’s really obvious.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve had those formal retraining meetings, and coworkers have had to step in and cover parts of what I was supposed to finish. And that makes me feel awful, because I don’t want people thinking they have to babysit me on the line.\n\nI guess in my personal life it bothers me mostly because I carry that stress home. I keep thinking, what if they move me off the line, or what if I get fired? I feel like I’m starting to look like a safety concern instead of someone reliable, and that’s… yeah, that really gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with tasks and memory have significantly affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I can point to, like, a specific friendship ending or my family saying I’m impossible to deal with because of it.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just more stressed when I’m around them, because I’ll be thinking about work in the background and then I’m not totally present. Like my mom will be talking and I’ll realize I was half listening, but then I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nSo… I guess it affects me, but I don’t know if it’s significantly affected the relationships themselves. I haven’t really asked them, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your difficulties with tasks and memory are significantly impacting your ability to interact with others or perform daily activities outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to measure that. Like, outside of work I still do things, I’m not just… completely unable to function or anything. But I do get kind of scattered, and then I start thinking about work again, and then I’m not really paying attention to whoever I’m with.

Like the other day—well, not the other day, but sometimes I’ll be with my roommate or on the phone with my mom, and I’m listening, but then I’m thinking about whether I forgot something on the line or if someone had to fix my mistake, and suddenly I missed half of what they said.

But I don’t know if that’s a daily activity problem exactly. I guess I’m not sure. It feels like work is where it really shows up, and everything else is more like… spillover from being stressed about it.
